NOTI OFCCLE AIM. Written notice of claim must be
given to the Insurance Company within 30 days after the
occurrence or start of the loss on which claim is based.
If notice is not given in that time, the claim will not be
invalidated or reduced if itis shown that written notice was
 given as soon as was reasonable possible.
 CLAIM FORMS. When the Insurance Company receives
 the notice of claim, it will give to the claimant, or to the
 Policyholder for claimant, the claim forms it used for filing
 proof of loss. If the claimant does not get these claim forms
 within 15 days after the Insurance Company receives notice
 of claim, he will be considered to have met the proof or loss
 requirements if he submits written proof of loss within 90
 days after the date of loss. This proof must describe the
 occurrence, character and extent of the loss for which claim

15(d). One of the reasons for the adoption of paragraph (d)
is that otherwise a plaintiff might be forced into *‘commenc-

commofe the n origic nal ae ctiom n have e man de ct lear the
right to relief.’’ Fed. R. Civ. P. 15 advisory committee

   1. I am Vice President - TelecommunicafotriHounmasna
Inc. and have been responsible for arranging local and long                                           /s/ William E. Lawrence

aries for eleven (11) years.

   2. I have reviewed the Plaintiff's Fifth Request for Pro-
duction of Documents in the case styled Mary Forsyth, et al.
vs. Humana Inc., et al., Case No. CV-S-89-249-PMP _
(LRL).

   3. Long distance telephone records of Humana Inc. for
the period 1984 throu1g9h88, incltu hosd e riecn ordg s of
long distance calls made between the corporate office of
Humana Inc. in Louisville, Kentucky and Humana Hospital-
Sunrise and Humana Health Insurance of Nevada’s market
office, both located in Las Vegas, Nevada, are maintained in
the form of copies of bills or invoices from various long
distance carriers, and are in storage in Louisville, Kentucky.

   4. In preparation to respond to Plaintiff's Fifth Request
for Production of Documents, I reviewed several selected
months’ internal reports summarizing Humana’s long dis-
tance telephone usage. Based upon my analysis of those
records, I have estimated that for the period 1984 through
1988, approximately fourteen million (14,000,000) long
distance telephone calls were made from Humana’s corpo-

and other states, including calls made to Humana Hospital-

to other locations in Nevada and to locations in other states,

 fl. STATEMENT OF FACTS

   On March29, 1989, Plaintiffs filed their original com-
 plaint with this Court, alleging claims under the Employee
 Retirement Income Security Act (‘‘ERISA*’), the Sherman
 Antitrust Act and various provisions of state law. On
 Septembe2r7, 1989, the Court dismissed Plaintiffs’ state
 law claims and directed them to file an Amended Complaint
 re-asserting the ERISA and antitrust counts. Plaintiffs filed
 the Amended Complaint on October 27, 1989, alleging

 claims under ERISA and section 2 of the Sherman Antitrust

   Moreover, Plaintiffs’ addition of a state RICO claim and
federal RICO conspiracy claims to this class action requires
a new class certification procedure pursuant to Fed. R. Civ.

P. 23(c)(1). See Zenith Laboratories, Inc. v. Carter-

Wallace, Inc., 64 F.R.D. 159, 164 (D.N.J. 1974), aff'd,
530 F.2d 508 (3d Cir.), cert. denied, 429 U.S. 828 (1976).
If these new claims were certified for class action treatment,
class members would require a new notice of their right to
opt out of the litigatSieoenF.ed. R. Civ. P. 23(c)(2); Mata-
razzo v. Friendly Ice Cream Corp., 70 F.R.D. 536, 560
(E.D.N.Y. 1976). The delay caused by this procedure
warrants the denial of leave to amend. See Matarazzo, 70
F.R.D. at 560 (leave to amend denied because of delay

caused by need to deliver new opt-out notice).

   The specter that Defendants have raised of ‘‘requir[ing]
Defendants to return their defense to square one,”’ Opposi-
tion at 5, is not only a chimera; it represents a monumental
hypocrisy. By far the greatest discovery burden during the
course of this litigation has fallen and continues to fall on
Plaintiffs, not on Defendants. It is Plaintiffs, not Defend-
ants, who have every reason to want this matter concluded
promptly. It is Plaintiffs, not Defendants, who have repeat-
edly sought to move this matter along to a final resolution.
Conversely, it is Defendants, not Plaintiffs, who have
pursued an ongoing strategy of endless delay and dilatory
tactics, and whose discovery abuses have even prompted this
Court to impose monetary sanctions. When the parties filed
a Joint Discovery Conference Report this past September 18,
it was Plaintiffs who requested an end to discovery within
six months, while it was Defendants who requested an addi-
tional year. The Court then set a discovery deadline of
January 31, 1992, and Plaintiffs have no intention of at-
tempting to pursue discovery beyond that date. Any efforts
at additional delay in this litigation will emanate from
Defendants, not from Plaintiffs. Moreover, given that
Defendants had initially sought an additional twelve months
for discovery, their ardent defense of the four-month dead-
line this Court imposed reveals itseif as nothing more than
